**Poland**,[^cite_note-18] officially the **Republic of Poland**,[^cite_note-19] is a country in [Central Europe](5188.md). It extends from the [Baltic Sea](3335.md) in the north to the [Sudetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudetes) and [Carpathian Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpathian_Mountains) in the south, and borders [Lithuania](17675.md) and [Russia](25391.md)[^cite_note-20] to the northeast; [Belarus](3457.md) and [Ukraine](31750.md) to the east; [Slovakia](26830.md) and the [Czech Republic](5321.md) to the south; and [Germany](11867.md) to the west. The territory has a varied landscape, diverse ecosystems, and a [temperate climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_climate). Poland is composed of sixteen [voivodeships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voivodeships_of_Poland) and is the fifth most populous [member state of the European Union](10890716.md) (EU), with over 38 million people, and the [fifth largest EU country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_European_countries_by_area) by land area, covering 312,696 km<sup>2</sup> (120,733 sq mi). The capital and [largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_and_towns_in_Poland) is [Warsaw](32908.md); other major cities include [Kraków](16815.md), [Wrocław](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wroc%C5%82aw), [Łódź](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%81%C3%B3d%C5%BA), [Poznań](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pozna%C5%84), and [Gdańsk](12099.md).&#x20;

[Prehistoric human activity on Polish soil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory_and_protohistory_of_Poland) dates to the [Lower Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Paleolithic), with continuous settlement since the end of the [Last Glacial Period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Glacial_Period). Culturally diverse throughout [late antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_antiquity), in the [early medieval period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Middle_Ages) the region became inhabited by the [West Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Slavs) tribal [Polans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polans_\(western\)), who gave [Poland its name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_Poland). The process of establishing [statehood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statehood) coincided with the conversion of a [pagan ruler of the Polans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mieszko_I) to [Christianity](5211.md) in 966 under the auspices of the [Catholic Church](606848.md). The [Kingdom of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Poland) emerged in 1025, and in 1569 cemented its long-standing [association with Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_union), thus forming the [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth). At the time, the Commonwealth was one of the [great powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_modern_great_powers) of Europe, with [an elective monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elective_monarchy_in_Poland) and [a uniquely liberal political system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Liberty), which adopted [Europe's first modern constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_3_May_1791) in 1791.&#x20;

With the passing of the prosperous [Polish Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Golden_Age), the country was [partitioned by neighbouring states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partitions_of_Poland) at the end of the 18th century. At the end of [World War I](4764461.md) in 1918, Poland regained [its independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Independence_Day_\(Poland\)) with the [founding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Poland_\(1795-1918\)#Recovery_of_statehood) of the [Second Polish Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Polish_Republic), which emerged [victorious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Warsaw_\(1920\)) in [various conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_wars_involving_Poland#Second_Polish_Republic_\(1918%E2%80%931939\)) of the [interbellum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Poland_\(1918%E2%80%931939\)) period. In September 1939, the [invasion of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Poland) by [Germany](21212.md) and [the invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_invasion_of_Poland) by the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) marked the beginning of [World War II](32927.md), which resulted in [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust_in_Poland) and millions of [Polish casualties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Poland_\(1939%E2%80%931945\)) as well as [extensive border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_evolution_of_Poland) and demographic changes. Forced into the [Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc) in the global [Cold War](325329.md), the [Polish People's Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_People%27s_Republic) was a signatory of the [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact). Through [the 1980 emergence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gda%C5%84sk_Agreement) and contributions of the [Solidarity movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solidarity_\(Polish_trade_union\)), which initiated the [fall of the Iron Curtain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Iron_Curtain), the [communist government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_United_Workers%27_Party) was [dissolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Poland_\(1945%E2%80%931989\)) and Poland as the first of the [communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism) countries re-established itself as a [liberal democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy) in 1989, and [held free elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Polish_parliamentary_election).&#x20;

Poland is a [parliamentary republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_republic) with its [bicameral legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicameralism) comprising the [Sejm](28167.md) and the [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Poland). Considered a [middle power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power) and a [regional power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_power), it is a [developed country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country) with a [developed market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_market) and [high-income economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Bank_high-income_economy). In terms of nominal GDP, it is the [21st largest economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) in the world and the sixth in the European Union, and [fifth largest by PPP-adjusted GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)) in the EU. Poland has free [university education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Poland) and [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_in_Poland). It has [18](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Poland) UNESCO [World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Sites), over 100 [Historic Monuments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Historic_Monuments_\(Poland\)) and 23 [national parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Parks_of_Poland). Poland is a founding member of the [United Nations](31769.md), and a member of the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe), [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization), [OECD](33853117.md), [NATO](21133.md), and [EU](9317.md) (including the [Schengen Area](16565139.md)).&#x20;

## Etymology

Main article: [Names of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_Poland)

The native [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language) name for Poland is *Polska*.[^cite_note-21] It is believed that the name derives from the [Polans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polans_\(western\)), a [West Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Slavs) tribe who inhabited the [Warta River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warta_River) basin of present-day [Greater Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Poland) region (6th–8th century CE).[^cite_note-22] The tribe's name stems from the [Proto-Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Slavic) noun *pole*, meaning field, which itself originates from the [Proto-Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Indo-European_language) word *\*pleh₂-* indicating flatland.[^cite_note-23] The etymology alludes to the [topography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topography) of the region and the flat landscape of Greater Poland.[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25] During the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), the [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin) form *Polonia* was widely used throughout Europe.[^cite_note-Buko_2014-26]&#x20;

The country's alternative archaic name is *[Lechia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lechia)* and its root syllable remains in official use in several languages, notably [Hungarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_language), [Lithuanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_language), and [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language).[^cite_note-27] The [exonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exonym) possibly derives from either [Lech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lech_and_Czech), a legendary ruler of the [Lechites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lechites), or from the [Lendians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lendians), a West Slavic tribe that dwelt on the south-easternmost edge of [Lesser Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesser_Poland).[^cite_note-Dabrowski_2014-28][^cite_note-29] The origin of the tribe's name lies in the [Old Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Polish_language) word *lęda* (plain).[^cite_note-30] Initially, both names *Lechia* and *Polonia* were used interchangeably when referring to Poland by chroniclers during the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages).[^cite_note-31]&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Poland)

### Prehistory and protohistory

Main articles: [Stone Age Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stone_Age_Poland), [Bronze and Iron Age Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_and_Iron_Age_Poland), [Poland in antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poland_in_antiquity), [Early Slavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Slavs), [West Slavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Slavs), [Lechites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lechites), and [Poland in the Early Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poland_in_the_Early_Middle_Ages)



Reconstruction of an 8th-century BCE [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age) [Lusatian-culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lusatian_culture) settlement in [Biskupin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biskupin)

The first [Stone Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stone_Age) archaic humans and *[Homo erectus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_erectus)* species settled what was to become Poland approximately 500,000 years ago, though the ensuing hostile climate prevented early humans from founding more permanent encampments.[^cite_note-32] The arrival of *[Homo sapiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_sapiens)* and [anatomically modern humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_European_modern_humans) coincided with the climatic discontinuity at the end of the [Last Glacial Period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Glacial_Period) ([Northern Polish glaciation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weichselian_glaciation) 10,000 BC), when Poland became habitable.[^cite_note-33] [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) excavations indicated broad-ranging development in that era; the earliest evidence of European cheesemaking (5500 BC) was discovered in Polish [Kuyavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuyavia),[^cite_note-34] and the [Bronocice pot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronocice_pot) is incised with the earliest known depiction of what may be a wheeled vehicle (3400 BC).[^cite_note-35]&#x20;

The period spanning the [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age) and the [Early Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Iron_Age) (1300 BC–500 BC) was marked by an increase in population density, establishment of [palisaded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palisade) settlements ([gords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gord_\(archaeology\))) and the expansion of [Lusatian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lusatian_culture).[^cite_note-36][^cite_note-37] A significant archaeological find from [the protohistory of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory_and_protohistory_of_Poland) is a fortified settlement at [Biskupin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biskupin), attributed to the Lusatian culture of the [Late Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Bronze_Age) (mid-8th century BC).[^cite_note-38]&#x20;

Throughout [antiquity](252905.md) (400 BC–500 AD), many distinct ancient populations inhabited the territory of present-day Poland, notably [Celtic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celts), [Scythian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scythia), [Germanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_peoples), [Sarmatian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarmatians), [Baltic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balts) and [Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Slavs) tribes.[^cite_note-39] Furthermore, archaeological findings confirmed the presence of [Roman Legions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Legions) sent to protect the [amber trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amber_Road).[^cite_note-40] The [Polish tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_tribes) emerged following the [second wave of the Migration Period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migration_Period#Second_wave) around the 6th century AD;[^cite_note-Buko_2014-26] they were [Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavs) and may have included assimilated remnants of peoples that earlier dwelled in the area.[^cite_note-41][^cite_note-42] Beginning in the early 10th century, the [Polans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polans_\(western\)) would come to dominate other [Lechitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lechites) tribes in the region, initially forming a tribal federation and later a centralised monarchical state.[^cite_note-43]&#x20;

### Kingdom of Poland

Main articles: [History of Poland during the Piast dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Poland_during_the_Piast_dynasty), [Baptism of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism_of_Poland), and [Kingdom of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Poland)



Poland under the rule of Duke [Mieszko I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mieszko_I), whose acceptance of Christianity under the auspices of the [Roman Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church) and the [Baptism of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism_of_Poland) marked the beginning of statehood in 966. His son, [Bolesław I the Brave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boles%C5%82aw_I_the_Brave), was crowned [king](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Poland) in 1025

Poland began to form into a recognisable unitary and territorial entity around the middle of the 10th century under the [Piast dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piast_dynasty).[^cite_note-44] In 966, the ruler of the Polans, [Mieszko I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mieszko_I_of_Poland), accepted [Western Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Christianity) under the auspices of the [Roman Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church) which was tantamount to the adoption of [Christianity by Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianization_of_Poland).[^cite_note-45] In 968, a missionary [bishopric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bishopric) was established in [Poznań](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pozna%C5%84). An [incipit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incipit) titled [Dagome iudex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dagome_iudex) first defined Poland's geographical boundaries with its capital in [Gniezno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gniezno) and affirmed that its monarchy was under the protection of the [Apostolic See](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_See).[^cite_note-Curta_2016-46] The country's early origins were described by [Gallus Anonymus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallus_Anonymus) in *[Gesta principum Polonorum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gesta_principum_Polonorum)*, the oldest Polish chronicle.[^cite_note-47] An important national event of the period was the [martyrdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martyrdom) of [Saint Adalbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adalbert_of_Prague), who was killed by [Prussian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Prussians) pagans in 997 and whose remains were reputedly bought back for their weight in gold by Mieszko's successor, [Bolesław I the Brave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boles%C5%82aw_I_the_Brave).[^cite_note-Curta_2016-46]&#x20;

In 1000, at the [Congress of Gniezno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Gniezno), Bolesław I the Brave obtained the right of [investiture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Investiture) from [Otto III, Holy Roman Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_III,_Holy_Roman_Emperor), who assented to the creation archdioceses in Gniezno[^cite_note-Curta_2016-46] and three new dioceses on Polish lands in [Kraków](16815.md), [Kołobrzeg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ko%C5%82obrzeg), and [Wrocław](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wroc%C5%82aw).[^cite_note-48] Also, Otto bestowed upon Bolesław royal [regalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regalia) and a replica of the [Holy Lance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Lance), which were later used at his coronation as the first [King of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Polish_monarchs) c. 1025, when Bolesław received permission for his coronation from [Pope John XIX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_John_XIX).[^cite_note-49][^cite_note-50] Bolesław also expanded the realm considerably by seizing parts of German [Lusatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lusatia), Czech [Moravia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moravia), [Upper Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Hungary), and southwestern regions of the [Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kievan_Rus%27).[^cite_note-51]&#x20;



[Casimir III the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casimir_III_the_Great) is the only Polish king to receive the title of *Great*. He built extensively during his reign, and reformed the Polish army along with the country's legal code, 1333–1370.

The transition from [paganism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_paganism) in Poland was not instantaneous and resulted in the [pagan reaction of the 1030s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pagan_reaction_in_Poland).[^cite_note-52] In 1031, [Mieszko II Lambert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mieszko_II_Lambert) lost the title of king and fled amidst the violence.[^cite_note-53] The unrest led to the transfer of the capital to Kraków in 1038 by [Casimir I the Restorer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casimir_I_the_Restorer).[^cite_note-54] In 1076, [Bolesław II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boles%C5%82aw_II_the_Generous) re-instituted the office of king, but was banished in 1079 for murdering his opponent, [Bishop Stanislaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanislaus_of_Szczepan%C3%B3w).[^cite_note-55] In 1138, the country [fragmented](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Testament_of_Boles%C5%82aw_III_Wrymouth) into five principalities when [Bolesław III Wrymouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boles%C5%82aw_III_Wrymouth) divided his lands among his sons.[^cite_note-Dabrowski_2014-28] These were [Lesser Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesser_Poland), Greater Poland, [Silesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silesia), [Masovia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masovia), and [Sandomierz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandomierz), with intermittent hold over [Pomerania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pomerania).[^cite_note-56] In 1226, [Konrad I of Masovia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konrad_I_of_Masovia) invited the [Teutonic Knights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teutonic_Knights) to aid in combating the [Baltic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balts) Prussians; a decision that later led to centuries of warfare with the Knights.[^cite_note-57]&#x20;

In the first half of the 13th century, [Henry I the Bearded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_I_the_Bearded) and [Henry II the Pious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_II_the_Pious) aimed to unite the fragmented dukedoms, but the [Mongol invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Mongol_invasion_of_Poland) and the death of Henry II in [battle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Legnica) hindered the unification.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59] As a result of the devastation which followed, depopulation and the demand for craft labour spurred a migration of [German and Flemish settlers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Germans_in_Poland) into Poland, which was encouraged by the Polish dukes.[^cite_note-60] In 1264, the [Statute of Kalisz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Kalisz) introduced unprecedented autonomy for the [Polish Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Poland), who came to Poland fleeing persecution elsewhere in Europe.[^cite_note-61]&#x20;

In 1320, [Władysław I the Short](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W%C5%82adys%C5%82aw_I_the_Elbow-high) became the first king of [a reunified Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Poland_\(1025%E2%80%931385\)) since [Przemysł II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Przemys%C5%82_II) in 1296,[^cite_note-62] and the first to be crowned at [Wawel Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wawel_Cathedral) in Kraków.[^cite_note-63] Beginning in 1333, the reign of [Casimir III the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casimir_III_the_Great) was marked by developments in [castle infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trail_of_the_Eagle%27s_Nests), army, judiciary and [diplomacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Krak%C3%B3w).[^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65] Under his authority, Poland transformed into a major European power; he instituted Polish rule over [Ruthenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Ruthenia) in 1340 and imposed quarantine that prevented the spread of [Black Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death).[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67] In 1364, Casimir inaugurated the [University of Kraków](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagiellonian_University), one of the oldest institutions of higher learning in Europe.[^cite_note-68] Upon his death in 1370, the Piast dynasty came to an end.[^cite_note-69] He was succeeded by his closest male relative, [Louis of Anjou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_I_of_Hungary), who ruled Poland, [Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hungary), and [Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatia_in_personal_union_with_Hungary) in a [personal union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_union).[^cite_note-Davies_2001-70] Louis' younger daughter [Jadwiga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jadwiga_of_Poland) became Poland's first female monarch in [1384](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1384).[^cite_note-Davies_2001-70]&#x20;

### The Golden Age

Main articles: [History of Poland during the Jagiellonian dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Poland_during_the_Jagiellonian_dynasty) and [Polish Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Golden_Age)



The [Battle of Grunwald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Grunwald) was fought against the [German Order of Teutonic Knights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teutonic_Knights), and resulted in a decisive victory for the [Kingdom of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Poland_\(1385%E2%80%931569\)), 15 July 1410.

In 1386, Jadwiga of Poland entered a marriage of convenience with [Władysław II Jagiełło](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W%C5%82adys%C5%82aw_II_Jagie%C5%82%C5%82o), the [Grand Duke of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duke_of_Lithuania), thus forming the [Jagiellonian dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagiellonian_dynasty) and the [Polish–Lithuanian union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_union) which spanned the late [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages) and early [Modern Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_history).[^cite_note-71] The partnership between Poles and Lithuanians brought the vast multi-ethnic [Lithuanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Lithuania) territories into Poland's sphere of influence and proved beneficial for its inhabitants, who coexisted in one of the largest European [political entities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_union) of the time.[^cite_note-72]&#x20;

In the Baltic Sea region, the struggle of Poland and Lithuania with the [Teutonic Knights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teutonic_Knights) continued and culminated at the [Battle of Grunwald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Grunwald) in [1410](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1410), where a combined Polish–Lithuanian army inflicted a decisive victory against them.[^cite_note-Wyrozumski_1986-73] In 1466, after the [Thirteen Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Years%27_War_\(1454%E2%80%9366\)), king [Casimir IV Jagiellon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casimir_IV_Jagiellon) gave royal consent to the [Peace of Thorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Peace_of_Thorn_\(1466\)), which created the future [Duchy of Prussia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Prussia) under Polish suzerainty and forced the Prussian rulers to pay [tributes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homage_\(feudal\)).[^cite_note-Dabrowski_2014-28] The Jagiellonian dynasty also established dynastic control over the kingdoms of [Bohemia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Bohemia) ([1471](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1471) onwards) and Hungary.[^cite_note-Norman_Davies_1996-74] In the south, Poland confronted the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) (at the [Varna Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Varna)) and the [Crimean Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Khanate), and in the east helped Lithuania to combat [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Moscow).[^cite_note-Dabrowski_2014-28]&#x20;

Poland was developing as a [feudal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feudalism) state, with a predominantly agricultural economy and an increasingly powerful [landed nobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landed_nobility) that confined the population to private manorial farmstead known as [*folwarks*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folwark).[^cite_note-75] In 1493, [John I Albert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_I_Albert) sanctioned the creation of a [bicameral parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicameral_parliament) (the Sejm) composed of a lower house, the chamber of deputies, and an upper house, the chamber of senators.[^cite_note-76] The *[Nihil novi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nihil_novi)* act adopted by the Polish [General Sejm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Sejm) in 1505, transferred most of the [legislative power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislature) from the monarch to the parliament, an event which marked the beginning of the period known as [Golden Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Liberty), when the state was ruled by the seemingly free and equal [Polish nobles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Szlachta).[^cite_note-77]&#x20;



[Wawel Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wawel_Castle) in [Kraków](16815.md), seat of [Polish kings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Polish_monarchs) from 1038 until the capital was moved to [Warsaw](32908.md) in 1596

The 16th century saw [Protestant Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_Reformation) movements making deep inroads into Polish Christianity, which resulted in the establishment of policies promoting religious tolerance, unique in Europe at that time.[^cite_note-Knoll_2011-78] This tolerance allowed the country to avoid the religious turmoil and [wars of religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_wars_of_religion) that beset Europe.[^cite_note-Knoll_2011-78] In Poland, [Nontrinitarian Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nontrinitarianism) became the doctrine of the so-called [Polish Brethren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Brethren), who separated from their [Calvinist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvinism) denomination and became the co-founders of global [Unitarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitarianism).[^cite_note-79]&#x20;

The European [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance) evoked under [Sigismund I the Old](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigismund_I_the_Old) and [Sigismund II Augustus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigismund_II_Augustus) a sense of urgency in the need to promote a [cultural awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_in_Poland). During the [Polish Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Golden_Age), the nation's economy and culture flourished. The Italian-born [Bona Sforza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bona_Sforza), daughter of the [Duke of Milan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gian_Galeazzo_Sforza) and queen consort to Sigismund I, made considerable contributions to [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Poland), [cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_cuisine), language and court customs at [Wawel Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wawel_Castle).[^cite_note-Dabrowski_2014-28]&#x20;

### Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth

Main articles: [History of Poland in the Early Modern era (1569–1795)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Poland_in_the_Early_Modern_era_\(1569%E2%80%931795\)), [Crown of the Kingdom of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_the_Kingdom_of_Poland), and [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth)



The [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth) at its greatest extent in 1619. At that time it was the largest country in Europe

The [Union of Lublin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Lublin) of 1569 established the [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth), a unified federal state with an [elective monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elective_monarchy) that was largely governed by the nobility.[^cite_note-Butterwick_2021-80] The latter coincided with a period of prosperity. The Polish-dominated union thereafter became a leading power and a major cultural entity, exercising political control over parts of Central, [Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe), [Southeastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Europe) and Northern Europe. The Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth occupied approximately 1 million km<sup>2</sup> (390,000 sq mi) [at its peak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truce_of_Deulino) and was the largest state in Europe.[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82] Simultaneously, Poland imposed [Polonisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polonisation) policies in newly acquired territories which were met with resistance from ethnic and religious minorities.[^cite_note-Butterwick_2021-80]&#x20;

In [1573](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1573), [Henry de Valois of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_III_of_France), the first elected king, approbated the [Henrician Articles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henrician_Articles) which obliged future monarchs to respect the rights of nobles.[^cite_note-83] When he left Poland to become [King of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_French_monarchs), his successor, [Stephen Báthory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_B%C3%A1thory), led a successful [campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livonian_campaign_of_Stephen_B%C3%A1thory) in the [Livonian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livonian_War), granting Poland more [lands across the eastern shores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livonia) of the Baltic Sea.[^cite_note-84] State affairs were then headed by [Jan Zamoyski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Zamoyski), the [Crown Chancellor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor_\(Poland\)).[^cite_note-85] Stephen's successor, [Sigismund III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigismund_III_Vasa), defeated a rival [Habsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Habsburg) electoral candidate, [Archduke Maximilian III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximilian_III,_Archduke_of_Austria), in the [War of the Polish Succession (1587–1588)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Polish_Succession_\(1587%E2%80%931588\)). In [1592](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1592), Sigismund succeeded his father [John Vasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_III_of_Sweden), in [Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Sweden).[^cite_note-86] The [Polish-Swedish union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish-Swedish_union) endured until 1599, when he was [deposed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_against_Sigismund) by the Swedes.[^cite_note-87]&#x20;



King [John III Sobieski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_III_Sobieski) defeated the [Ottoman Turks](22278.md) at the [Battle of Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Vienna) on 12 September 1683.

In 1609, Sigismund [invaded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Muscovite_War_\(1605%E2%80%931618\)) [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsardom_of_Russia) which was engulfed in a [civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_of_Troubles),[^cite_note-Dabrowski_2014-28] and a year later the Polish [winged hussar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_hussars) units under [Stanisław Żółkiewski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanis%C5%82aw_%C5%BB%C3%B3%C5%82kiewski) [occupied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_occupation_of_Moscow) Moscow for two years after defeating the Russians at [Klushino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Klushino).[^cite_note-Dabrowski_2014-28] Sigismund also countered the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) in the southeast; at [Khotyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Khotyn_\(1621\)) in [1621](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1621) [Jan Karol Chodkiewicz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Karol_Chodkiewicz) achieved a decisive victory against the Turks, which ushered the downfall of Sultan [Osman II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osman_II).[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89] Sigismund's long reign in Poland coincided with the [Silver Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigismund_III_Vasa#Legacy).[^cite_note-90] The liberal [Władysław IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W%C5%82adys%C5%82aw_IV_Vasa) effectively defended Poland's territorial possessions but after his death the vast Commonwealth began declining from internal disorder and constant warfare.[^cite_note-91][^cite_note-92]&#x20;

In 1648, the Polish hegemony over Ukraine sparked the [Khmelnytsky Uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmelnytsky_Uprising),[^cite_note-93] followed by the decimating [Swedish Deluge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deluge_\(history\)) during the [Second Northern War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Northern_War),[^cite_note-Scott_2015-94] and Prussia's [independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Bromberg) in 1657.[^cite_note-Scott_2015-94] In 1683, [John III Sobieski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_III_Sobieski) re-established military prowess when he halted the advance of an [Ottoman Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Army_\(15th-19th_centuries\)) into Europe at the [Battle of Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Vienna).[^cite_note-95] The [Saxon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Wettin) era, under [Augustus II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augustus_II_the_Strong) and [Augustus III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augustus_III_of_Poland), saw neighbouring powers grow in strength at the expense of Poland. Both Saxon kings faced opposition from [Stanisław Leszczyński](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanis%C5%82aw_Leszczy%C5%84ski) during the [Great Northern War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Northern_War) (1700) and the [War of the Polish Succession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Polish_Succession) (1733).[^cite_note-96]&#x20;

### Partitions

Main articles: [History of Poland (1795–1918)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Poland_\(1795%E2%80%931918\)) and [Partitions of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partitions_of_Poland)



[Stanisław II Augustus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanis%C5%82aw_II_Augustus), the last [King of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Poland), reigned from 1764 until his abdication on 25 November 1795.

The [royal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_elections_in_Poland) of 1764 resulted in the elevation of [Stanisław II Augustus Poniatowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanis%C5%82aw_August_Poniatowski) to the monarchy.[^cite_note-97] His candidacy was extensively funded by his sponsor and former lover, Empress [Catherine II of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_II_of_Russia).[^cite_note-98] The new king maneuvered between his desire to implement necessary modernising reforms, and the necessity to remain at peace with surrounding states.[^cite_note-99] His ideals led to the formation of the 1768 [Bar Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar_Confederation), a rebellion directed against the Poniatowski and all external influence, which ineptly aimed to preserve Poland's sovereignty and privileges held by the nobility.[^cite_note-100] The failed attempts at government restructuring as well as the domestic turmoil provoked its neighbours to invade.[^cite_note-101]&#x20;

In 1772, the [First Partition of the Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Partition_of_Poland) by Prussia, Russia, and Austria took place, an act which the [Partition Sejm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_Sejm), under considerable duress, eventually ratified as a [fait accompli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_French_words_and_phrases_used_by_English_speakers#F).[^cite_note-Gierowski_1986-102] Disregarding the territorial losses, in 1773 a plan of critical reforms was established, in which the [Commission of National Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commission_of_National_Education), the first government education authority in Europe, was inaugurated.[^cite_note-103] Corporal punishment of schoolchildren was officially prohibited in 1783. Poniatowski was the head figure of the [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Enlightenment), encouraged the development of industries, and embraced republican [neoclassicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture).[^cite_note-104] For his contributions to the arts and sciences, he was awarded a [Fellowship of the Royal Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fellowship_of_the_Royal_Society).[^cite_note-105]&#x20;

In 1791, the [Great Sejm parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Sejm) adopted the [3 May Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_May_3,_1791), the first set of supreme national laws, and introduced a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy).[^cite_note-106] The [Targowica Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Targowica_Confederation), an organisation of nobles and deputies opposing the act, appealed to Catherine and caused the [1792 Polish–Russian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Russian_War_of_1792).[^cite_note-107] Fearing the reemergence of Polish hegemony, Russia and Prussia arranged and, in 1793, executed the [Second Partition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Partition_of_Poland), which left the country deprived of territory and incapable of independent existence. On 24 October 1795, the Commonwealth was [partitioned for the third time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Partition_of_Poland) and ceased to exist as a territorial entity.[^cite_note-Gierowski-108][^cite_note-109] Stanisław Augustus, the last King of Poland, abdicated the throne on 25 November 1795.[^cite_note-110]&#x20;

### Era of insurrections

Main articles: [Austrian Partition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_Partition), [Prussian Partition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prussian_Partition), and [Russian Partition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Partition)



The [partitions of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partitions_of_Poland), carried out by the [Kingdom of Prussia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Prussia) (*blue*), the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) (*brown*), and the [Austrian Habsburg Monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg_monarchy) (*green*) in [1772](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Partition_of_Poland), [1793](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Partition_of_Poland) and [1795](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Partition_of_Poland)

The Polish people [rose several times against the partitioners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_wars_involving_Poland) and occupying armies. An unsuccessful attempt at defending Poland's sovereignty took place in the 1794 [Kościuszko Uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ko%C5%9Bciuszko_Uprising), where a popular and distinguished general [Tadeusz Kościuszko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tadeusz_Ko%C5%9Bciuszko), who had several years earlier served under [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington) in the [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War), led Polish insurgents.[^cite_note-Storozynski_2009-111] Despite the victory at the [Battle of Racławice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Rac%C5%82awice), his ultimate defeat ended Poland's independent existence [for 123 years](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Partition_of_Poland).[^cite_note-112]&#x20;

In 1806, an [insurrection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Poland_uprising_\(1806\)) organised by [Jan Henryk Dąbrowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Henryk_D%C4%85browski) liberated western Poland ahead of [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_I_of_France)'s advance into Prussia during the [War of the Fourth Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Fourth_Coalition). In accordance with the 1807 [Treaty of Tilsit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Tilsit), Napoleon proclaimed the [Duchy of Warsaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Warsaw), a [client state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Client_state) ruled by his ally [Frederick Augustus I of Saxony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Augustus_I_of_Saxony). The Poles actively aided French troops in the [Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars), particularly those under [Józef Poniatowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B3zef_Poniatowski) who became [Marshal of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshal_of_the_Empire) shortly before his death at [Leipzig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Leipzig) in 1813.[^cite_note-113] In the aftermath of Napoleon's exile, the Duchy of Warsaw was abolished at the [Congress of Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Vienna) in 1815 and its territory was divided into Russian [Congress Kingdom of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_Kingdom_of_Poland), the Prussian [Grand Duchy of Posen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Posen), and [Austrian Galicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_Poland) with the [Free City of Kraków](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_City_of_Krak%C3%B3w).[^cite_note-114]&#x20;

&#x20;



[Tadeusz Kościuszko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tadeusz_Ko%C5%9Bciuszko) was a veteran and hero of both the [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Russian_War_of_1792) and [American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War) wars of independence.

[^cite_note-Storozynski_2009-111]

In 1830, [non-commissioned officers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-commissioned_officer) at Warsaw's [Officer Cadet School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corps_of_Cadets_\(Warsaw\)) rebelled in what was the [November Uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/November_Uprising).[^cite_note-115] After its collapse, Congress Poland lost its [constitutional autonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Congress_Poland), [army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_of_Congress_Poland) and legislative assembly.[^cite_note-116] During the [European Spring of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spring_of_Nations), Poles took up arms in the [Greater Poland Uprising of 1848](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Poland_Uprising_\(1848\)) to resist [Germanisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanisation), but its failure saw the duchy's status reduced to a mere [province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Posen); and subsequent integration into the [German Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire) in 1871.[^cite_note-117] In Russia, the fall of the [January Uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_Uprising) (1863–1864) prompted severe [political, social and cultural reprisals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_Uprising#The_decades_of_reprisals), followed by deportations and [pogroms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pogroms) of the Polish-Jewish population. Towards the end of the 19th century, Congress Poland became heavily industrialised; its primary exports being coal, [zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc), iron and textiles.[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-119]&#x20;

### Second Polish Republic

Main articles: [History of Poland (1918–1939)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Poland_\(1918%E2%80%931939\)), [Battle of Warsaw (1920)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Warsaw_\(1920\)), and [Second Polish Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Polish_Republic)



[Chief of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naczelnik_pa%C5%84stwa) Marshal [Józef Piłsudski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B3zef_Pi%C5%82sudski) was a hero of the Polish independence campaign and the nation's premiere statesman from 1918 until his death on 12 May 1935.

In the aftermath of [World War I](4764461.md), the [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I) agreed on the reconstitution of Poland, confirmed through the [Treaty of Versailles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Versailles) of June 1919.[^cite_note-120] A total of 2 million Poles served in the armies of the German Empire, Austria-Hungary, and the Russian Empire, and over 450,000 died.[^cite_note-121] Following the [armistice with Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armistice_with_Germany_\(Compi%C3%A8gne\)) in November 1918, Poland regained its independence as the [Second Polish Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Polish_Republic).[^cite_note-122]&#x20;

The Second Polish Republic reaffirmed its sovereignty after [a series of military conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_wars_involving_Poland), most notably the [Polish–Soviet War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Soviet_War), when Poland inflicted a crushing defeat on the [Red Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army) at the [Battle of Warsaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Warsaw_\(1920\)).[^cite_note-123]&#x20;

The inter-war period heralded a new era of Polish politics. Whilst Polish political activists had faced heavy censorship in the decades up until [World War I](4764461.md), a new political tradition was established in the country. Many exiled Polish activists, such as [Ignacy Jan Paderewski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ignacy_Jan_Paderewski), who would later become prime minister, returned home. A significant number of them then went on to take key positions in the newly formed political and governmental structures. Tragedy struck in 1922 when [Gabriel Narutowicz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_Narutowicz), inaugural holder of the presidency, was assassinated at the [Zachęta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zach%C4%99ta) Gallery in Warsaw by a painter and right-wing nationalist [Eligiusz Niewiadomski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eligiusz_Niewiadomski).[^cite_note-Bitter_glory-124]&#x20;

In 1926, the [May Coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_Coup_\(Poland\)), led by the hero of the Polish independence campaign Marshal [Józef Piłsudski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B3zef_Pi%C5%82sudski), turned rule of the Second Polish Republic over to the nonpartisan [Sanacja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanacja) (*Healing*) movement to prevent radical political organisations on both the left and the right from destabilising the country.[^cite_note-125] By the late 1930s, due to increased threats posed by political extremism inside the country, the Polish government became increasingly heavy-handed, banning a number of radical organisations, including communist and ultra-nationalist political parties, which threatened the stability of the country.[^cite_note-126]&#x20;

### World War II

Main articles: [History of Poland (1939–1945)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Poland_\(1939%E2%80%931945\)), [Invasion of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Poland), [Military history of Poland during World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_Poland_during_World_War_II), and [War crimes in occupied Poland during World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_crimes_in_occupied_Poland_during_World_War_II)



[Polish Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Armed_Forces_\(Second_Polish_Republic\)) [7TP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7TP) tanks on military manoeuvres shortly before the [invasion of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Poland) in 1939

World War II began with the [Nazi German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_German) [invasion of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Poland) on 1 September 1939, followed by the [Soviet invasion of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_invasion_of_Poland) on 17 September. As agreed in the [Molotov–Ribbentrop Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molotov%E2%80%93Ribbentrop_Pact), Poland was split into two zones, [one occupied by Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_areas_annexed_by_Nazi_Germany), the other by [the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territories_of_Poland_annexed_by_the_Soviet_Union). The [wartime resistance movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_resistance_movement_in_World_War_II), and the [Armia Krajowa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armia_Krajowa) (*Home Army*), fought against the occupation. It was one of the three largest resistance movements of the entire war, and encompassed a range of clandestine activities, which functioned as an [underground state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Underground_State) complete with [degree-awarding universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Poland_during_World_War_II) and [a court system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Underground_court).[^cite_note-Stanislaw_Salmonowicz_1994-127] The resistance was loyal to the [Polish Government in Exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Government_in_Exile) and generally resented the idea of a post-war communist Poland; for this reason, in the summer of 1944 it initiated [Operation Tempest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Tempest), of which the [Warsaw Uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Uprising) that began on 1 August 1944 is the best-known operation.[^cite_note-Lerski_1996b-128][^cite_note-polandinexile-129]&#x20;

Poland made the fourth-largest troop contribution in Europe,[^cite_note-Zaloga_1982-130][^cite_note-Lerski_1996a-131][^cite_note-Walters_1988-132] and its troops served both the exiled government in the [west](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Armed_Forces_in_the_West) and Soviet leadership in the [east](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Armed_Forces_in_the_East). Polish troops played an important role in the [Normandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Overlord), [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Campaign_\(World_War_II\)), [North African Campaigns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_African_Campaign) and [Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Pheasant) and are particularly remembered for the [Battle of Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Britain) and [Battle of Monte Cassino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Monte_Cassino).[^cite_note-tobruk-133][^cite_note-including-134] Polish intelligence operatives proved extremely valuable to the Allies, providing much of the intelligence from Europe and beyond,[^cite_note-135] [Polish code breakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Cipher_Bureau) were responsible for [cracking the Enigma cipher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cryptanalysis_of_the_Enigma) and Polish scientists participating in the [Manhattan Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manhattan_Project) were co-creators of the American [atomic bomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_bomb). In the east, the Soviet-backed [Polish 1st Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Polish_Army_\(1944%E2%80%931945\)) distinguished itself in the battles for [Warsaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Uprising) and [Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Berlin).[^cite_note-Lerski_1996b-128]&#x20;



Map of [the Holocaust in German-occupied Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust_in_occupied_Poland) with marked deportation routes, major [ghettos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ghettos_in_German-occupied_Poland) and Nazi [extermination camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extermination_camps). The border in 1941 between [Nazi Germany](21212.md) and the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) is marked in red.

The actions undertaken by both occupiers within Polish territory were [genocidal](12441.md). In 1939–1941, the Soviets deported hundreds of thousands of Poles and the Soviet [NKVD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NKVD) executed thousands of Polish [prisoners of war](25008.md) ahead of [Operation Barbarossa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Barbarossa), notably in the [Katyn massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katyn_massacre).[^cite_note-BBC_2010-136] Around 150,000 Polish civilians were killed by Soviets during the Soviet Union's occupation of eastern Poland ([Kresy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kresy)). Similarly, German planners had in November 1939 called for "the complete destruction of all Poles" and their fate as outlined in the *[Generalplan Ost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generalplan_Ost)*;[^cite_note-137] six German [extermination camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extermination_camp) were established in occupied Poland, including [Treblinka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treblinka_extermination_camp), [Majdanek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majdanek_concentration_camp) and [Auschwitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auschwitz_concentration_camp). The Germans [transported millions of Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_train) from across occupied Europe to be murdered in those camps.[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139] Altogether, 3 million Polish Jews[^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141] – approximately 90% of Poland's pre-war Jewry – and between 1.8 and 2.8 million ethnic Poles[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143][^cite_note-144] were killed, including up to 100,000 members of the Polish [intelligentsia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligentsia). The [Romani people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people) were also exterminated.[^cite_note-145] During the [Warsaw Uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Uprising) alone, over 150,000 Polish civilians were killed, most were murdered by the Germans during the [Wola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wola_massacre) and [Ochota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ochota_massacre) massacres.[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147] At least 100,000 Poles were also murdered by the [Ukrainian Insurgent Army](1045217.md) (UPA) between 1943 and 1944 in what became known as the [Wołyń Massacres](464698.md).[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149]&#x20;

[Of all the countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_casualties) in the war, Poland lost the highest percentage of its citizens: around 6 million perished – more than one-sixth of Poland's pre-war population – [half of them](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_in_Poland) Polish Jews.[^cite_note-150][^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152] About 90% of deaths were non-military in nature.[^cite_note-153] In 1945, Poland's borders [were shifted westwards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_changes_of_Poland_immediately_after_World_War_II). The shift forced the migration of [millions of people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_evacuation_and_expulsion).[^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155][^cite_note-BBC_2011-156] Over two million Polish inhabitants of [Kresy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kresy) [were expelled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_population_transfers_\(1944%E2%80%931946\)) along the [Curzon Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curzon_Line) by [Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin).[^cite_note-157] Up to eight million [Germans were expelled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_and_expulsion_of_Germans_from_Poland_during_and_after_World_War_II) by Poland from the [territories it acquired](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Former_eastern_territories_of_Germany).[^cite_note-158] The western border became the [Oder-Neisse line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oder-Neisse_line). As a result, Poland's territory was reduced by 20%, or 77,500 square kilometres (29,900 sq mi).&#x20;

### Post-war communism

Main articles: [History of Poland (1945–1989)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Poland_\(1945%E2%80%931989\)), [Polish People's Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_People%27s_Republic), [History of Solidarity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Solidarity), and [Polish Round Table Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Round_Table_Agreement)



*At [High Noon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Noon), 4 June 1989*—political poster featuring [Gary Cooper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Cooper) to encourage votes for the [Solidarity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solidarity_\(Polish_trade_union\)) party in the [1989 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contract_Sejm)

At the insistence of [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin), the [Yalta Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yalta_Conference) sanctioned the formation of a new provisional pro-Communist coalition government in Moscow, which ignored the [Polish government-in-exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_government-in-exile) based in London. This action angered many Poles who considered it a [betrayal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yalta_betrayal) by the Allies.[^cite_note-159] In 1944, Stalin had made guarantees to [Churchill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Churchill) and [Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt) that he would maintain Poland's sovereignty and allow democratic elections to take place. However, upon achieving victory in 1945, the elections organised by the occupying Soviet authorities were falsified and were used to provide a veneer of legitimacy for Soviet hegemony over Polish affairs.[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161][^cite_note-162] The Soviet Union instituted a new [communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_state) government in Poland, analogous to much of the rest of the [Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc). [As elsewhere in Communist Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Communism_in_the_Communist_Bloc), the Soviet influence over Poland was met with [armed resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cursed_soldiers) from the outset which continued into the 1950s.[^cite_note-163]&#x20;

Despite widespread objections, the new Polish government accepted the Soviet annexation of the pre-war eastern regions of Poland[^cite_note-Lane_1948-164] (in particular the cities of [Wilno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilno) and [Lwów](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lw%C3%B3w)) and agreed to the permanent garrisoning of [Red Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army) units on Poland's territory. Military alignment within the [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact) throughout the [Cold War](325329.md) came about as a direct result of this change in Poland's political culture. In the European scene, it came to characterise the full-fledged integration of Poland into the brotherhood of communist nations.[^cite_note-165]&#x20;

The new communist government took control with the adoption of the [Small Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_Constitution_of_1947) on 19 February 1947. The [Polish People's Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_People%27s_Republic) (*Polska Rzeczpospolita Ludowa*) [was officially proclaimed in 1952](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_People%27s_Republic_of_Poland).[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167] In 1956, after the death of [Bolesław Bierut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boles%C5%82aw_Bierut), the régime of [Władysław Gomułka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W%C5%82adys%C5%82aw_Gomu%C5%82ka) became temporarily more liberal, freeing many people from prison and expanding some personal freedoms. [Collectivisation in the Polish People's Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collectivization_in_the_Polish_People%27s_Republic) failed. A similar situation repeated itself in the 1970s under [Edward Gierek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Gierek), but most of the time persecution of [anti-communist opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communist_resistance_in_Poland_\(1944%E2%80%931989\)) groups persisted. Despite this, Poland was at the time considered to be one of the least oppressive states of the Eastern Bloc.[^cite_note-PWN-168]&#x20;

Labour turmoil in 1980 led to the formation of the independent trade union "[Solidarity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solidarity_\(Polish_trade_union\))" ("*Solidarność*"), which over time became a political force.[^cite_note-169] Despite persecution and imposition of [martial law in 1981](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_law_in_Poland) by General [Wojciech Jaruzelski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wojciech_Jaruzelski), it eroded the dominance of the [Polish United Workers' Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_United_Workers%27_Party) and by 1989 had triumphed in Poland's first [partially free and democratic parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contract_Sejm) since the end of the Second World War. [Lech Wałęsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lech_Wa%C5%82%C4%99sa), a Solidarity candidate, eventually [won the presidency in 1990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_Polish_presidential_election).[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172] The Solidarity movement heralded the [collapse of communist regimes and parties across Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutions_of_1989).[^cite_note-173]&#x20;

### Third Polish Republic

Main article: [History of Poland (1989–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Poland_\(1989%E2%80%93present\))



Flowers in front of the [Presidential Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Palace,_Warsaw) following the [death of Poland's top government officials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Polish_Air_Force_Tu-154_crash) in a plane crash on 10 April 2010

A [shock therapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shock_therapy_\(economics\)) programme, initiated by [Leszek Balcerowicz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leszek_Balcerowicz) in the early 1990s, enabled the country to transform its [Soviet-style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet-style_socialism) [planned economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_economy) into a [market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_economy).[^cite_note-174] As with other [post-communist countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-communism), Poland suffered temporary declines in social, economic, and living standards,[^cite_note-175] but it became the first post-communist country to reach its pre-1989 [GDP levels](12594.md) as early as 1995, although the unemployment rate increased.[^cite_note-176] Poland became a member of the [Visegrád Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visegr%C3%A1d_Group) in 1991,[^cite_note-177] and joined [NATO](21133.md) in 1999.[^cite_note-178] Poles then voted to join the [European Union](9317.md) in [a referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Polish_European_Union_membership_referendum) in June 2003,[^cite_note-179] with [Poland becoming a full member](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poland_in_the_European_Union) on 1 May 2004, following the [consequent enlargement of the union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_enlargement_of_the_European_Union).[^cite_note-180]&#x20;

Poland joined the [Schengen Area](16565139.md) in 2007, as a result of which, [the country's borders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borders_of_Poland) with other member states of the [European Union](9317.md) were dismantled, allowing for [full freedom of movement](1270497.md#European_Union) within most of the European Union.[^cite_note-BBC_News_2007-181] On 10 April 2010, the [President of Poland](43581.md) [Lech Kaczyński](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lech_Kaczy%C5%84ski), along with 89 other high-ranking Polish officials [died in a plane crash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Polish_Air_Force_Tu-154_crash) near [Smolensk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smolensk), Russia.[^cite_note-182]&#x20;

In 2011, the ruling [Civic Platform](23998.md) won [parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Polish_parliamentary_election).[^cite_note-183] In 2014, the [Prime Minister of Poland](40543.md), [Donald Tusk](648724.md), was chosen to be [President of the European Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_European_Council), and resigned as prime minister.[^cite_note-184] The [2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Polish_parliamentary_election) and [2019 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Polish_parliamentary_election) were won by the national-conservative [Law and Justice](520309.md) Party (PiS) led by [Jarosław Kaczyński](637440.md),[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186] resulting in increased [Euroscepticism](10037.md) and [increased friction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_constitutional_crisis) with the European Union.[^cite_note-187] In December 2017, [Mateusz Morawiecki](48505487.md) was sworn in as the Prime Minister, succeeding [Beata Szydlo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beata_Szyd%C5%82o), in office since 2015. President [Andrzej Duda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrzej_Duda), supported by Law and Justice party, was re-elected in the 2020 presidential [election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Polish_presidential_election).[^cite_note-188]&#x20;

As of November 2023, the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) had led to 17 million [Ukrainian refugees](70199806.md) crossing the border to Poland.[^cite_note-UNHRC_Ukraine-189] As of November 2023, 0.9 million of those had stayed in Poland.[^cite_note-UNHRC_Ukraine-189] In October 2023, the ruling Law and Justice (PiS) party won the largest share of the vote in the [election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Polish_parliamentary_election), but lost its majority in parliament. In December 2023, Donald Tusk became the new Prime Minister leading a coalition made up of [Civic Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civic_Coalition_\(Poland\)), [Third Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Way_\(Poland\)), and [The Left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Left_\(Poland\)). Law and Justice became the leading opposition party.[^cite_note-190]&#x20;

On 1 June 2025, The Law and Justice candidate for the [2025 Polish presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Polish_presidential_election), [Karol Nawrocki](69581936.md) won the election, beating [Civic Platform](23998.md) candidate [Rafał Trzaskowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafa%C5%82_Trzaskowski). The results continued the streak of Law and Justice aligned presidential candidates only losing one presidential election in [2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Polish_presidential_election) since its founding in 2001.[^cite_note-191]&#x20;

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Poland)



[Topographic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topography) map of Poland

Poland covers an administrative area of 312,722 km<sup>2</sup> (120,743 sq mi), and is the [ninth-largest country in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_European_countries_by_area). Approximately 311,895 km<sup>2</sup> (120,423 sq mi) of the country's territory consists of land, 2,041 km<sup>2</sup> (788 sq mi) is internal waters and 8,783 km<sup>2</sup> (3,391 sq mi) is territorial sea.[^cite_note-Rocznik_2019-192] Topographically, the landscape of Poland is characterised by diverse [landforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landform), [water bodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrology) and [ecosystems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecosystem).[^cite_note-193] The central and northern region bordering the [Baltic Sea](3335.md) lie within the flat [Central European Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_European_Plain), but its south is hilly and mountainous.[^cite_note-194] The average [elevation above the sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Height_above_sea_level) is estimated at 173 metres.[^cite_note-Rocznik_2019-192]&#x20;

The country has a coastline spanning 770 km (480 mi); extending from the shores of the Baltic Sea, along the [Bay of Pomerania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay_of_Pomerania) in the west to the [Gulf of Gdańsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gda%C5%84sk_Bay) in the east.[^cite_note-Rocznik_2019-192] The beach coastline is abundant in [sand dune fields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sand_dunes) or [coastal ridges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beach_ridge) and is indented by [spits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spit_\(landform\)) and lagoons, notably the [Hel Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hel_Peninsula) and the [Vistula Lagoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vistula_Lagoon), which is shared with Russia.[^cite_note-195] The largest Polish island on the Baltic Sea is [Wolin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolin), located within [Wolin National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolin_National_Park).[^cite_note-196] Poland also shares the [Szczecin Lagoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Szczecin_Lagoon) and the [Usedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usedom) island with Germany.[^cite_note-197]&#x20;



[Morskie Oko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morskie_Oko) alpine lake in the [Tatra Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatra_Mountains), part of the [Carpathian massif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpathian_Mountains)

The mountainous belt in the extreme south of Poland is divided into two major [mountain ranges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_ranges); the [Sudetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudetes) in the west and the [Carpathians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpathian_Mountains) in the east. The highest part of the Carpathian massif are the [Tatra Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatra_Mountains), extending along Poland's southern border.[^cite_note-198] Poland's highest point is the northwestern summit of [Rysy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rysy) at 2,500 metres (8,202 ft) in elevation, located in the Tatras.[^cite_note-199] The highest summit of the Sudetes massif is [Mount Śnieżka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Anie%C5%BCka) at 1,603.3 metres (5,260 ft), shared with the Czech Republic.[^cite_note-200] The lowest point in Poland situated at [Marzęcino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marz%C4%99cino) on the [Vistula Fens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vistula_Fens), which is 2.2 metres (7.2 ft) below sea level.[^cite_note-201]&#x20;

In Poland, [Trójmorski Wierch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tr%C3%B3jmorski_Wierch) is one of the six tripoints of the [European watershed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_watershed).&#x20;

Poland's [longest rivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rivers_of_Poland) are the [Vistula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vistula_River), the [Oder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oder_River), the [Warta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warta), and the [Bug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bug_River).[^cite_note-Rocznik_2019-192] The country also possesses one of the highest densities of lakes in the world, numbering around ten thousand and mostly concentrated in the north-eastern region of [Masuria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masuria), within the [Masurian Lake District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masurian_Lake_District).[^cite_note-202] The largest lakes, covering more than 100 square kilometres (39 sq mi), are [Śniardwy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Aniardwy) and [Mamry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Mamry), and the deepest is [Lake Hańcza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ha%C5%84cza) at 108.5 metres (356 ft) in depth.[^cite_note-Rocznik_2019-192]&#x20;

### Climate

Main article: [Geography of Poland § Climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Poland#Climate)



[Köppen-Geiger climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) map of Poland

The climate of Poland is [temperate transitional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperateness), and varies from [oceanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate) in the north-west to [continental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_climate) in the south-east.[^cite_note-Korzeniewska_2020-203] The mountainous southern fringes are situated within an [alpine climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpine_climate).[^cite_note-Korzeniewska_2020-203] Poland is characterised by warm summers, with a mean temperature of around 20 °C (68.0 °F) in July, and moderately cold winters averaging −1.2 °C (29.8 °F) in January.[^cite_note-204] In summer, maximum temperatures can reach 40 °C (104.0 °F), while in winter in the Tatra Mountains and Bieszczady Mountains they can even drop below −30 °C (−22.0 °F).&#x20;

The warmest and sunniest part of Poland is [Lower Silesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Silesia) in the southwest and the coldest region is the northeast corner, around [Suwałki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suwa%C5%82ki) and [Olecko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olecko) where the climate is affected by [cold fronts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_front) from [Scandinavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavia) and [Siberia](29639.md).[^cite_note-Anna_Chodubska_2020-205] [Precipitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precipitation_\(meteorology\)) is more frequent during the summer months, with highest rainfall recorded from June to September.[^cite_note-Azad_2021-206]&#x20;

There is a considerable fluctuation in day-to-day weather and the arrival of a particular season can differ each year.[^cite_note-Korzeniewska_2020-203] [Climate change](5042951.md) and other factors have further contributed to interannual [thermal anomalies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperature_anomaly) and increased temperatures; the average annual air temperature between 2011 and 2020 was 9.33 °C (48.8 °F), around 1.11 °C higher than in the 2001–2010 period.[^cite_note-Anna_Chodubska_2020-205] Winters are also becoming increasingly drier, with less [snowfall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snowfall).[^cite_note-Korzeniewska_2020-203]&#x20;

### Biodiversity

Main article: [Geography of Poland § Biodiversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Poland#Biodiversity)



The [European bison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_bison) (*żubr*), one of [Poland's national animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_symbols_of_Poland), is commonly found at the ancient and [UNESCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Poland)-protected [Białowieża Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bia%C5%82owie%C5%BCa_Forest).

[Phytogeographically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phytogeography), Poland belongs to the Central European province of the [Circumboreal Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumboreal_Region) within the [Boreal Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boreal_Kingdom). The country has four [Palearctic ecoregions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palearctic_realm) – Central, Northern, Western European [temperate broadleaf and mixed forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_broadleaf_and_mixed_forest), and the [Carpathian montane conifer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpathian_montane_conifer_forests). Forests occupy 31% of Poland's land area, the largest of which is the [Lower Silesian Wilderness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Silesian_Wilderness).[^cite_note-207] The most common [deciduous trees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deciduous_trees) found across the country are [oak](39696.md), [maple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maple), and [beech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beech); the most common conifers are [pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pine), [spruce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spruce), and [fir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fir).[^cite_note-208] An estimated 69% of all forests are [coniferous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coniferous).[^cite_note-209]&#x20;

The [flora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flora) and [fauna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna_of_Poland) in Poland is that of [Continental Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Europe), with the [wisent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_bison), [white stork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_stork) and [white-tailed eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-tailed_eagle) designated as national animals, and the [red common poppy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papaver_rhoeas) being the unofficial floral emblem.[^cite_note-210] Among the most protected species is the [European bison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_bison), Europe's heaviest land animal, as well as the [Eurasian beaver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_beaver), the [brown bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_bear), the [northern lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_lynx), the [Eurasian wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_wolf) and the [Tatra chamois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatra_chamois).[^cite_note-Rocznik_2019-192] The region was also home to the extinct [aurochs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurochs), the last individual dying in Poland in 1627.[^cite_note-211] Game animals such as [red deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_European_red_deer), [roe deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_deer), and [wild boar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_European_boar) are found in most woodlands.[^cite_note-212] Poland is also a significant breeding ground for [migratory birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migratory_birds) and hosts around one quarter of the global population of white storks.[^cite_note-213]&#x20;
